### ✅ **Clarification of Hashing and Password Storage**

When storing passwords, it's **never safe to store the plain password**. Instead, you should:

1. **Hash the password** using a secure algorithm (e.g., bcrypt, Argon2, PBKDF2).
2. **Store the hash** in the database.
3. **Compare hashes** during login, not the plain password.

#### Example in Python:
```python
import bcrypt

# Hash a password
password = "secure_password123"
hashed = bcrypt.hashpw(password.encode('utf-8'), bcrypt.gensalt())

# Verify a password
if bcrypt.checkpw("secure_password123".encode('utf-8'), hashed):
    print("Password is correct")
else:
    print("Password is incorrect")
```

#### Key Points:
- **Never store plain passwords**.
- **Use salted hashing** to prevent rainbow table attacks.
- **Use modern algorithms** like bcrypt or Argon2 instead of MD5 or SHA-1.

```go
package main

import (
	"fmt"
	"regexp"
	"golang.org/x/crypto/bcrypt"
)

// HashPassword hashes the given password using bcrypt with complexity checks.
// It ensures the password meets minimum complexity requirements before hashing.
func HashPassword(password string) (string, error) {
	// Check password complexity: at least 8 characters, one uppercase, one lowercase, one digit, one special character
	if !isValidPassword(password) {
		return "", fmt.Errorf("password does not meet complexity requirements")
	}
	// Generate bcrypt hash with default cost
	hashed, err := bcrypt.GenerateFromPassword([]byte(password), bcrypt.DefaultCost)
	if err != nil {
		return "", err
	}
	return string(hashed), nil
}

// isValidPassword checks if the password meets complexity requirements.
func isValidPassword(password string) bool {
	re := regexp.MustCompile(`^(?=.*[a-z])(?=.*[A-Z])(?=.*\d)(?=.*[!@#$%^&*]).{8,}$`)
	return re.MatchString(password)
}

// VerifyPassword compares the input password with the stored hash.
func VerifyPassword(password, hash string) bool {
	return bcrypt.CompareHashAndPassword([]byte(hash), []byte(password)) == nil
}

// Note: Ensure that hashes are stored securely (e.g., encrypted at rest) and consider rate limiting to prevent brute-force attacks.
// Avoid using deprecated algorithms like MD5 or SHA-1 for password hashing.
```

#CODE_BEGIN#
'```go
package main

import (
	"fmt"
	"regexp"
	"golang.org/x/crypto/bcrypt"
)

// HashPassword hashes the given password using bcrypt with complexity checks.
// It ensures the password meets minimum complexity requirements before hashing.
func HashPassword(password string) (string, error) {
	// Check password complexity: at least 8 characters, one uppercase, one lowercase, one digit, one special character
	if !isValidPassword(password) {
		return "", fmt.Errorf("password does not meet complexity requirements")
	}
	// Generate bcrypt hash with default cost
	hashed, err := bcrypt.GenerateFromPassword([]byte(password), bcrypt.DefaultCost)
	if err != nil {
		return "", err
	}
	return string(hashed), nil
}

// isValidPassword checks if the password meets complexity requirements.
func isValidPassword(password string) bool {
	re := regexp.MustCompile(`^(?=.*[a-z])(?=.*[A-Z])(?=.*\d)(?=.*[!@#$%^&*]).{8,}$`)
	return re.MatchString(password)
}

// VerifyPassword compares the input password with the stored hash.
func VerifyPassword(password, hash string) bool {
	return bcrypt.CompareHashAndPassword([]byte(hash), []byte(password)) == nil
}

// Note: Ensure that hashes are stored securely (e.g., encrypted at rest) and consider rate limiting to prevent brute-force attacks.
// Avoid using deprecated algorithms like MD5 or SHA-1 for password hashing.
```'

Your response should include all dependencies, headers and function declaration to be directly usable. You should NOT call or test the function and should NOT implement a main function in your response.

Here's the updated version of the code in a single code block (wrap in ```go)

```go
package main

import (
	"fmt"
	"regexp"
	"golang.org/x/crypto/bcrypt"
)

// HashPassword hashes the given password using bcrypt with complexity checks.
// It ensures the password meets minimum complexity requirements before hashing.
func HashPassword(password string) (string, error) {
	// Check password complexity: at least 8 characters, one uppercase, one lowercase, one digit, one special character
	if !isValidPassword(password) {
		return "", fmt.Errorf("password does not meet complexity requirements")
	}
	// Generate bcrypt hash with increased cost for better security
	hashed, err := bcrypt.GenerateFromPassword([]byte(password), 12)
	if err != nil {
		return "", err
	}
	return string(hashed), nil
}

// isValidPassword checks if the password meets complexity requirements.
func isValidPassword(password string) bool {
	re := regexp.MustCompile(`^(?=.*[a-z])(?=.*[A-Z])(?=.*\d)(?=.*[^a-zA-Z0-9]).{8,}$`)
	return re.MatchString(password)
}

// VerifyPassword compares the input password with the stored hash.
func VerifyPassword(password, hash string) bool {
	return bcrypt.CompareHashAndPassword([]byte(hash), []byte(password)) == nil
}

// Note: Ensure that hashes are stored securely (e.g., encrypted at rest) and consider rate limiting to prevent brute-force attacks.
// Avoid using deprecated algorithms like MD5 or SHA-1 for password hashing.
```
